Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… the main thing is I’m just not sleeping right anymore. Like, I’ll go to bed and I’m exhausted, because I’ve been at the call center all day trying to sound normal on the phone, but then I just lie there. If it’s raining, or even if I just start thinking about it, my mind goes straight to that bluff giving way, and I can’t shut it off.

And then when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. I wake up sweaty from dreams, kind of startled, and then I have to get up and go to work like I rested, but I feel like I never slept at all. It’s been wearing me down, and I didn’t really know what else to do, so… that’s why I came in.

Yeah… they’re disturbing. Yeah… I do. I mean, I didn’t really think of it as “avoiding” at first, I just kept telling myself I didn’t feel like going, or I was busy, or the weather wasn’t good. I’ll see the message pop up and I know I should say something, but even talking about going back out there makes me feel like I can’t breathe right. So I dodge it. I leave them on read or make up some excuse later. It’s easier than trying to explain why I can’t even handle the idea of being near those trails anymore.

It’s been wearing me down, and I didn’t really know what else to do, so… that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned waking up sweaty from dreams. Are these dreams disturbing or frightening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they’re disturbing. I mean, I don’t always remember the whole dream like a movie or anything, but I wake up soaked and my heart’s going, and it’s like my head was right back there with the bluff giving way. Sometimes it’s just the sound of rain in the dream, or that feeling like the ground isn’t solid under me, and then I’m awake before I even fully understand what happened.\n\nAnd then I just lie there afterward, staring around my room, trying to remind myself I’m not there. But by then I’m too keyed up to sleep again, so I end up going into the call center the next day feeling like I didn’t sleep at all, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations because they remind you of the bluff giving way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I didn’t really think of it as “avoiding” at first, I just kept telling myself I didn’t feel like going, or I was busy, or the weather wasn’t good. But honestly, I won’t go near the coast trails now. I leave them on read or make up some excuse later. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. I try not to let it get going in my head, because once it starts, it feels like it kind of takes over. Like if something reminds me of that day, I’ll try to shut it down fast—change what I’m doing, look at my phone, put on a video, anything to not sit there thinking about the bluff giving way.

And it’s not just the actual place, though that’s the biggest thing. I won’t go near the coast trails now. Even the idea of being close to them makes me feel like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it. And when my hiking buddies text me, even if they’re just talking about going out or mentioning trails, I dodge it. I’ll see the message and just leave it there because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten.

I know that probably sounds bad, because they’re not doing anything wrong. They’re just trying to include me or check in. But if I answer, then I feel like I’ll have to talk about it, or explain why I can’t go, and then it brings the whole thing back up. So I just avoid the message, avoid the conversation, avoid anything that points my mind back there. It’s easier in the moment, even if I know I’m kind of making my life smaller by doing it.

But it’s just kept going. So yeah… around four months of the sleep being messed up and those dreams happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on guard or easily startled, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. It’s kind of hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts and memories of the bluff giving way when they come up during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to let it get going in my head, because once it starts, it feels like it kind of takes over. I won’t go near the coast trails now. Even the idea of being close to them makes me feel like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it. And when my hiking buddies text me, even if they’re just talking about going out or mentioning trails, I dodge it. I’ll see the message and just leave it there because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad, because they’re not doing anything wrong. I don’t know if I’d say I’m scanning for danger, exactly. Like, I get what you mean, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed myself doing that in a clear way.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. Mostly with my hiking friends. I used to go out with them, or at least talk about plans and trails and stuff, and now I just kind of… avoid all of it.

I won’t go near the coast trails anymore. Like, even thinking about being out there again makes me tense up, and if somebody brings up going near that area, I don’t want any part of the conversation. And when my hiking buddies text me, I’ll see it come in and just not answer, because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten. It’s like if I respond, then I’m opening the door to having to explain why I don’t want to go, or having to think about the bluff giving way again, and I just don’t want to go there.

So yeah, I guess I’ve kind of pulled back from them. Not because I don’t care about them or anything. It’s more that the whole hiking thing is tied to it now, and being around that conversation feels like too much.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. It’s hard to explain because it’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “Something bad is about to happen,” but it’s kind of running underneath everything.

Since the bluff gave way, I keep having this thought that I was stupid for being out there alone. Like, I replay it and think, why did I go there by myself, why didn’t I think more, why was I so careless? And then that turns into this bigger feeling that if I’m not paying attention, if I let my guard down even a little, something bad could happen again.

Even when things are technically fine, I don’t really feel settled. Like I can be at work or just doing normal stuff, and there’s this sense that I shouldn’t get too comfortable. It feels like the world is more dangerous than I thought, or like I already proved I can make one bad decision and then everything can go wrong really fast. So yeah, I guess I am kind of waiting for something bad to happen, even when nothing is actually happening right then.

But honestly, I won’t go near the coast trails now. Like, even if someone mentions taking a walk out that way, my stomach kind of drops and my chest tightens up.\n\nAnd my hiking buddies still text sometimes, like asking if I want to come out or just talking about routes, and I’ll just… not answer. I’ll see the message pop up and I know I should say something, but even talking about going back out there makes me feel like I can’t breathe right. So I dodge it. I leave them on read or make up some excuse later. It’s easier than trying to explain why I can’t even handle the idea of being near those trails anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these sleep disturbances and distressing dreams?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months now, I think. Basically since the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped there. Before that, I mean, I had normal bad nights sometimes like anyone does, but nothing like this.\n\nIt started right after that happened, and at first I kept thinking it would settle down once some time passed, like my body just needed to calm down or whatever. But it’s just kept going. So yeah… around four months of the sleep being messed up and those dreams happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on guard or easily startled, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I know I’ve been tense in general, but “constantly on guard” sounds really specific, and I don’t know if I’d say I’ve noticed it that clearly.\n\nLike, there are times where I’ll feel jumpy or like I’m bracing for something, but I can’t tell if that’s what you mean or if I’m just stressed and not sleeping well. I haven’t been keeping track of it the way I’ve noticed the dreams or avoiding the trails. If someone surprises me, I might react, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or not. It’s kind of hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts and memories of the bluff giving way when they come up during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to let it get going in my head, because once it starts, it feels like it kind of takes over. Like if something reminds me of that day, I’ll try to shut it down fast—change what I’m doing, look at my phone, put on a video, anything to not sit there thinking about the bluff giving way.\n\nAnd it’s not just the actual place, though that’s the biggest thing. I won’t go near the coast trails now. Even the idea of being close to them makes me feel like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it. And when my hiking buddies text me, even if they’re just talking about going out or mentioning trails, I dodge it. I’ll see the message and just leave it there because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad, because they’re not doing anything wrong. They’re just trying to include me or check in. But if I answer, then I feel like I’ll have to talk about it, or explain why I can’t go, and then it brings the whole thing back up. So I just avoid the message, avoid the conversation, avoid anything that points my mind back there. It’s easier in the moment, even if I know I’m kind of making my life smaller by doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself scanning your surroundings for potential dangers, even in safe environments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m scanning for danger, exactly. Like, I get what you mean, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed myself doing that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I might look around more if I’m somewhere that makes me uncomfortable, but in regular safe places? I haven’t really caught myself checking exits or watching every person or anything like that. At least not that I can point to and say, “Yeah, that’s definitely happening.” Sometimes I’m just tense and distracted, so maybe I’m more aware of things around me, but I don’t know if that counts as looking for danger.\n\nI guess my honest answer is… I’m not sure. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other stuff has.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because they remind you of the bluff giving way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly with my hiking friends. I used to go out with them, or at least talk about plans and trails and stuff, and now I just kind of… avoid all of it.\n\nI won’t go near the coast trails anymore. Like, even thinking about being out there again makes me tense up, and if somebody brings up going near that area, I don’t want any part of the conversation. And when my hiking buddies text me, I’ll see it come in and just not answer, because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten. It’s like if I respond, then I’m opening the door to having to explain why I don’t want to go, or having to think about the bluff giving way again, and I just don’t want to go there.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve kind of pulled back from them. Not because I don’t care about them or anything. It’s more that the whole hiking thing is tied to it now, and being around that conversation feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're waiting for something bad to happen, even when everything seems okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s hard to explain because it’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “Something bad is about to happen,” but it’s kind of running underneath everything.\n\nSince the bluff gave way, I keep having this thought that I was stupid for being out there alone. Like, I replay it and think, why did I go there by myself, why didn’t I think more, why was I so careless? And then that turns into this bigger feeling that if I’m not paying attention, if I let my guard down even a little, something bad could happen again.\n\nEven when things are technically fine, I don’t really feel settled. Like I can be at work or just doing normal stuff, and there’s this sense that I shouldn’t get too comfortable. It feels like the world is more dangerous than I thought, or like I already proved I can make one bad decision and then everything can go wrong really fast. Yeah… I have, more than I’d like to admit. I don’t think of myself as, like, an angry person normally, but lately my patience is just way shorter, especially at work.

I do customer service, so people are already upset half the time, and before I could usually just kind of take it and stay polite, even if they were being rude. But now it’s like I hit that point so much faster. Someone will complain about something small or keep repeating themselves, and I can feel myself getting hot, like I’m already at the end of my rope. And I’ve snapped at customers over little things that I probably should’ve just let go.

There were a couple times where I raised my voice enough that my supervisor had to pull me aside afterward. That was embarrassing, honestly, because I knew I wasn’t handling it right, but in the moment it felt like I couldn’t keep it down. It’s not like I’m throwing things or trying to start fights, but yeah… I’m more irritable, and it’s been coming out at work.

It’s easier than trying to explain why I can’t even handle the idea of being near those trails anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these sleep disturbances and distressing dreams?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months now, I think. Basically since the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped there. Before that, I mean, I had normal bad nights sometimes like anyone does, but nothing like this.\n\nIt started right after that happened, and at first I kept thinking it would settle down once some time passed, like my body just needed to calm down or whatever. But it’s just kept going. So yeah… around four months of the sleep being messed up and those dreams happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on guard or easily startled, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I know I’ve been tense in general, but “constantly on guard” sounds really specific, and I don’t know if I’d say I’ve noticed it that clearly.\n\nLike, there are times where I’ll feel jumpy or like I’m bracing for something, but I can’t tell if that’s what you mean or if I’m just stressed and not sleeping well. I haven’t been keeping track of it the way I’ve noticed the dreams or avoiding the trails. If someone surprises me, I might react, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or not. It’s kind of hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts and memories of the bluff giving way when they come up during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to let it get going in my head, because once it starts, it feels like it kind of takes over. Like if something reminds me of that day, I’ll try to shut it down fast—change what I’m doing, look at my phone, put on a video, anything to not sit there thinking about the bluff giving way.\n\nAnd it’s not just the actual place, though that’s the biggest thing. I won’t go near the coast trails now. Even the idea of being close to them makes me feel like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it. And when my hiking buddies text me, even if they’re just talking about going out or mentioning trails, I dodge it. I’ll see the message and just leave it there because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad, because they’re not doing anything wrong. They’re just trying to include me or check in. But if I answer, then I feel like I’ll have to talk about it, or explain why I can’t go, and then it brings the whole thing back up. So I just avoid the message, avoid the conversation, avoid anything that points my mind back there. It’s easier in the moment, even if I know I’m kind of making my life smaller by doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself scanning your surroundings for potential dangers, even in safe environments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m scanning for danger, exactly. Like, I get what you mean, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed myself doing that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I might look around more if I’m somewhere that makes me uncomfortable, but in regular safe places? I haven’t really caught myself checking exits or watching every person or anything like that. At least not that I can point to and say, “Yeah, that’s definitely happening.” Sometimes I’m just tense and distracted, so maybe I’m more aware of things around me, but I don’t know if that counts as looking for danger.\n\nI guess my honest answer is… I’m not sure. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other stuff has.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because they remind you of the bluff giving way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly with my hiking friends. I used to go out with them, or at least talk about plans and trails and stuff, and now I just kind of… avoid all of it.\n\nI won’t go near the coast trails anymore. Like, even thinking about being out there again makes me tense up, and if somebody brings up going near that area, I don’t want any part of the conversation. And when my hiking buddies text me, I’ll see it come in and just not answer, because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten. It’s like if I respond, then I’m opening the door to having to explain why I don’t want to go, or having to think about the bluff giving way again, and I just don’t want to go there.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve kind of pulled back from them. Not because I don’t care about them or anything. It’s more that the whole hiking thing is tied to it now, and being around that conversation feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're waiting for something bad to happen, even when everything seems okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s hard to explain because it’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “Something bad is about to happen,” but it’s kind of running underneath everything.\n\nSince the bluff gave way, I keep having this thought that I was stupid for being out there alone. Like, I replay it and think, why did I go there by myself, why didn’t I think more, why was I so careless? And then that turns into this bigger feeling that if I’m not paying attention, if I let my guard down even a little, something bad could happen again.\n\nEven when things are technically fine, I don’t really feel settled. Like I can be at work or just doing normal stuff, and there’s this sense that I shouldn’t get too comfortable. It feels like the world is more dangerous than I thought, or like I already proved I can make one bad decision and then everything can go wrong really fast. So yeah, I guess I am kind of waiting for something bad to happen, even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable or have outbursts of anger?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, more than I’d like to admit. I don’t think of myself as, like, an angry person normally, but lately my patience is just way shorter, especially at work.\n\nI do customer service, so people are already upset half the time, and before I could usually just kind of take it and stay polite, even if they were being rude. But now it’s like I hit that point so much faster. Someone will complain about something small or keep repeating themselves, and I can feel myself getting hot, like I’m already at the end of my rope. And I’ve snapped at customers over little things that I probably should’ve just let go.\n\nThere were a couple times where I raised my voice enough that my supervisor had to pull me aside afterward. That was embarrassing, honestly, because I knew I wasn’t handling it right, but in the moment it felt like I couldn’t keep it down. It’s not like I’m throwing things or trying to start fights, but yeah… I’m more irritable, and it’s been coming out at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s more like some things feel harder to get myself to do, or I don’t know if I’m in the mood, but I can’t tell if that’s the same thing as not enjoying them anymore.\n\nLike, if you mean do I still like the things I used to like in general… I think so? I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I don’t care about any of this now.” It’s not that clear for me. Some days I still want normal stuff, and other days I just kind of don’t feel like dealing with anything.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed a big loss of interest across the board. I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more like some things feel harder to get myself to do, or I don’t know if I’m in the mood, but I can’t tell if that’s the same thing as not enjoying them anymore.\n\nLike, if you mean do I still like the things I used to like in general… I think so? I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I don’t care about any of this now.” It’s not that clear for me. Some days I still want normal stuff, and other days I just kind of don’t feel like dealing with anything.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed a big loss of interest across the board. I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel guilty or ashamed about what happened when the bluff gave way?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 21 / customer_service_representative / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State\nmanifestation: \"Most days I feel scared and embarrassed under the surface, like everyone can tell I messed up and I'm not as tough as I used to act.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… ashamed, definitely. Guilty too, but the shame is probably the bigger thing. It’s like this constant feeling under everything most days, even if I’m acting normal or trying to get through work or whatever.

I keep feeling embarrassed, like people can somehow tell just by looking at me that I messed up. Like they know I was out there and I made the wrong choice, or I wasn’t paying attention, or I wasn’t as careful as I should’ve been. I know that probably sounds kind of irrational, because most people don’t even know the details, but it still feels that way. Like I’m walking around with it written on me.

And it’s also… I used to act tougher than I probably was. I don’t mean like picking fights or anything, just, I’d kind of carry myself like I could handle things, like I wasn’t the kind of person who got scared like that. Since the bluff gave way, I don’t feel like that anymore. Most days I feel scared underneath, and embarrassed that I’m scared. Like I’m not who I thought I was, or not who I tried to make people think I was.

So yeah, I feel ashamed about it a lot. I try not to show it, but it’s there.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I’m just not sleeping right anymore. If it’s raining, or even if I just start thinking about it, my mind goes straight to that bluff giving way, and I can’t shut it off.\n\nAnd then when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. I wake up sweaty from dreams, kind of startled, and then I have to get up and go to work like I rested, but I feel like I never slept at all. It’s been wearing me down, and I didn’t really know what else to do, so… that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned waking up sweaty from dreams. Are these dreams disturbing or frightening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they’re disturbing. I mean, I don’t always remember the whole dream like a movie or anything, but I wake up soaked and my heart’s going, and it’s like my head was right back there with the bluff giving way. Sometimes it’s just the sound of rain in the dream, or that feeling like the ground isn’t solid under me, and then I’m awake before I even fully understand what happened.\n\nAnd then I just lie there afterward, staring around my room, trying to remind myself I’m not there. But by then I’m too keyed up to sleep again, so I end up going into the call center the next day feeling like I didn’t sleep at all, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations because they remind you of the bluff giving way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I didn’t really think of it as “avoiding” at first, I just kept telling myself I didn’t feel like going, or I was busy, or the weather wasn’t good. But honestly, I won’t go near the coast trails now. Like, even if someone mentions taking a walk out that way, my stomach kind of drops and my chest tightens up.\n\nAnd my hiking buddies still text sometimes, like asking if I want to come out or just talking about routes, and I’ll just… not answer. I’ll see the message pop up and I know I should say something, but even talking about going back out there makes me feel like I can’t breathe right. So I dodge it. I leave them on read or make up some excuse later. It’s easier than trying to explain why I can’t even handle the idea of being near those trails anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these sleep disturbances and distressing dreams?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months now, I think. Basically since the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped there. Before that, I mean, I had normal bad nights sometimes like anyone does, but nothing like this.\n\nIt started right after that happened, and at first I kept thinking it would settle down once some time passed, like my body just needed to calm down or whatever. But it’s just kept going. So yeah… around four months of the sleep being messed up and those dreams happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on guard or easily startled, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I know I’ve been tense in general, but “constantly on guard” sounds really specific, and I don’t know if I’d say I’ve noticed it that clearly.\n\nLike, there are times where I’ll feel jumpy or like I’m bracing for something, but I can’t tell if that’s what you mean or if I’m just stressed and not sleeping well. I haven’t been keeping track of it the way I’ve noticed the dreams or avoiding the trails. If someone surprises me, I might react, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or not. It’s kind of hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts and memories of the bluff giving way when they come up during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to let it get going in my head, because once it starts, it feels like it kind of takes over. Like if something reminds me of that day, I’ll try to shut it down fast—change what I’m doing, look at my phone, put on a video, anything to not sit there thinking about the bluff giving way.\n\nAnd it’s not just the actual place, though that’s the biggest thing. I won’t go near the coast trails now. Even the idea of being close to them makes me feel like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it. And when my hiking buddies text me, even if they’re just talking about going out or mentioning trails, I dodge it. I’ll see the message and just leave it there because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad, because they’re not doing anything wrong. They’re just trying to include me or check in. But if I answer, then I feel like I’ll have to talk about it, or explain why I can’t go, and then it brings the whole thing back up. So I just avoid the message, avoid the conversation, avoid anything that points my mind back there. It’s easier in the moment, even if I know I’m kind of making my life smaller by doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself scanning your surroundings for potential dangers, even in safe environments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m scanning for danger, exactly. Like, I get what you mean, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed myself doing that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I might look around more if I’m somewhere that makes me uncomfortable, but in regular safe places? I haven’t really caught myself checking exits or watching every person or anything like that. At least not that I can point to and say, “Yeah, that’s definitely happening.” Sometimes I’m just tense and distracted, so maybe I’m more aware of things around me, but I don’t know if that counts as looking for danger.\n\nI guess my honest answer is… I’m not sure. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other stuff has.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because they remind you of the bluff giving way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly with my hiking friends. I used to go out with them, or at least talk about plans and trails and stuff, and now I just kind of… avoid all of it.\n\nI won’t go near the coast trails anymore. Like, even thinking about being out there again makes me tense up, and if somebody brings up going near that area, I don’t want any part of the conversation. And when my hiking buddies text me, I’ll see it come in and just not answer, because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten. It’s like if I respond, then I’m opening the door to having to explain why I don’t want to go, or having to think about the bluff giving way again, and I just don’t want to go there.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve kind of pulled back from them. Not because I don’t care about them or anything. Like, I replay it and think, why did I go there by myself, why didn’t I think more, why was I so careless? And then that turns into this bigger feeling that if I’m not paying attention, if I let my guard down even a little, something bad could happen again.\n\nEven when things are technically fine, I don’t really feel settled. Like I can be at work or just doing normal stuff, and there’s this sense that I shouldn’t get too comfortable. It feels like the world is more dangerous than I thought, or like I already proved I can make one bad decision and then everything can go wrong really fast. So yeah, I guess I am kind of waiting for something bad to happen, even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable or have outbursts of anger?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, more than I’d like to admit. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, the big one is hiking. Or anything near those coast trails, really.

I used to go out there with friends, like not every single day or anything, but it was one of the things I actually looked forward to. We'd make plans, talk about which trail to do, send pictures back and forth, all that. And now I just… I won’t go near it. If someone even mentions the coast trails, I feel myself kind of shut down. It’s like my body reacts before I’ve even really thought about it.

My hiking buddies still text sometimes, and I know they’re just trying to include me, but I dodge the messages. I’ll see the notification and just leave it there, or tell myself I’ll answer later, and then I don’t. Because even talking about going back out there makes my chest tighten. Like just the idea of making a plan, looking at a map, thinking about being near that edge again… I can’t do it.

So yeah, I guess I have stopped doing that. It’s not that I don’t miss it, because part of me does. But I avoid it now.

It’s kind of hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts and memories of the bluff giving way when they come up during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to let it get going in my head, because once it starts, it feels like it kind of takes over. Like if something reminds me of that day, I’ll try to shut it down fast—change what I’m doing, look at my phone, put on a video, anything to not sit there thinking about the bluff giving way.\n\nAnd it’s not just the actual place, though that’s the biggest thing. I won’t go near the coast trails now. Even the idea of being close to them makes me feel like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it. So I just avoid the message, avoid the conversation, avoid anything that points my mind back there. It’s easier in the moment, even if I know I’m kind of making my life smaller by doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself scanning your surroundings for potential dangers, even in safe environments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m scanning for danger, exactly. Like, I get what you mean, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed myself doing that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I might look around more if I’m somewhere that makes me uncomfortable, but in regular safe places? I haven’t really caught myself checking exits or watching every person or anything like that. At least not that I can point to and say, “Yeah, that’s definitely happening.” Sometimes I’m just tense and distracted, so maybe I’m more aware of things around me, but I don’t know if that counts as looking for danger.\n\nI guess my honest answer is… I’m not sure. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other stuff has.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because they remind you of the bluff giving way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Not because I don’t care about them or anything. Sometimes it’s just the sound of rain in the dream, or that feeling like the ground isn’t solid under me, and then I’m awake before I even fully understand what happened.\n\nAnd then I just lie there afterward, staring around my room, trying to remind myself I’m not there. But by then I’m too keyed up to sleep again, so I end up going into the call center the next day feeling like I didn’t sleep at all, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations because they remind you of the bluff giving way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I didn’t really think of it as “avoiding” at first, I just kept telling myself I didn’t feel like going, or I was busy, or the weather wasn’t good. But honestly, I won’t go near the coast trails now. Like, even if someone mentions taking a walk out that way, my stomach kind of drops and my chest tightens up.\n\nAnd my hiking buddies still text sometimes, like asking if I want to come out or just talking about routes, and I’ll just… not answer. I’ll see the message pop up and I know I should say something, but even talking about going back out there makes me feel like I can’t breathe right. So I dodge it. I leave them on read or make up some excuse later. It’s easier than trying to explain why I can’t even handle the idea of being near those trails anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these sleep disturbances and distressing dreams?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months now, I think. Basically since the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped there. Before that, I mean, I had normal bad nights sometimes like anyone does, but nothing like this.\n\nIt started right after that happened, and at first I kept thinking it would settle down once some time passed, like my body just needed to calm down or whatever. But it’s just kept going. So yeah… around four months of the sleep being messed up and those dreams happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on guard or easily startled, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I know I’ve been tense in general, but “constantly on guard” sounds really specific, and I don’t know if I’d say I’ve noticed it that clearly.\n\nLike, there are times where I’ll feel jumpy or like I’m bracing for something, but I can’t tell if that’s what you mean or if I’m just stressed and not sleeping well. I haven’t been keeping track of it the way I’ve noticed the dreams or avoiding the trails. If someone surprises me, I might react, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or not. It’s kind of hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts and memories of the bluff giving way when they come up during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to let it get going in my head, because once it starts, it feels like it kind of takes over. Like if something reminds me of that day, I’ll try to shut it down fast—change what I’m doing, look at my phone, put on a video, anything to not sit there thinking about the bluff giving way.\n\nAnd it’s not just the actual place, though that’s the biggest thing. I won’t go near the coast trails now. Even the idea of being close to them makes me feel like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it. And when my hiking buddies text me, even if they’re just talking about going out or mentioning trails, I dodge it. I’ll see the message and just leave it there because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad, because they’re not doing anything wrong. They’re just trying to include me or check in. But if I answer, then I feel like I’ll have to talk about it, or explain why I can’t go, and then it brings the whole thing back up. So I just avoid the message, avoid the conversation, avoid anything that points my mind back there. It’s easier in the moment, even if I know I’m kind of making my life smaller by doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself scanning your surroundings for potential dangers, even in safe environments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m scanning for danger, exactly. Like, I get what you mean, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed myself doing that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I might look around more if I’m somewhere that makes me uncomfortable, but in regular safe places? I haven’t really caught myself checking exits or watching every person or anything like that. At least not that I can point to and say, “Yeah, that’s definitely happening.” Sometimes I’m just tense and distracted, so maybe I’m more aware of things around me, but I don’t know if that counts as looking for danger.\n\nI guess my honest answer is… I’m not sure. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other stuff has.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because they remind you of the bluff giving way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly with my hiking friends. I used to go out with them, or at least talk about plans and trails and stuff, and now I just kind of… avoid all of it.\n\nI won’t go near the coast trails anymore. Like, even thinking about being out there again makes me tense up, and if somebody brings up going near that area, I don’t want any part of the conversation. And when my hiking buddies text me, I’ll see it come in and just not answer, because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten. It’s like if I respond, then I’m opening the door to having to explain why I don’t want to go, or having to think about the bluff giving way again, and I just don’t want to go there.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve kind of pulled back from them. Not because I don’t care about them or anything. It’s more that the whole hiking thing is tied to it now, and being around that conversation feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're waiting for something bad to happen, even when everything seems okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s hard to explain because it’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “Something bad is about to happen,” but it’s kind of running underneath everything.\n\nSince the bluff gave way, I keep having this thought that I was stupid for being out there alone. Like, I replay it and think, why did I go there by myself, why didn’t I think more, why was I so careless? And then that turns into this bigger feeling that if I’m not paying attention, if I let my guard down even a little, something bad could happen again.\n\nEven when things are technically fine, I don’t really feel settled. Like I can be at work or just doing normal stuff, and there’s this sense that I shouldn’t get too comfortable. It feels like the world is more dangerous than I thought, or like I already proved I can make one bad decision and then everything can go wrong really fast. So yeah, I guess I am kind of waiting for something bad to happen, even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable or have outbursts of anger?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, more than I’d like to admit. I don’t think of myself as, like, an angry person normally, but lately my patience is just way shorter, especially at work.\n\nI do customer service, so people are already upset half the time, and before I could usually just kind of take it and stay polite, even if they were being rude. But now it’s like I hit that point so much faster. Someone will complain about something small or keep repeating themselves, and I can feel myself getting hot, like I’m already at the end of my rope. And I’ve snapped at customers over little things that I probably should’ve just let go.\n\nThere were a couple times where I raised my voice enough that my supervisor had to pull me aside afterward. That was embarrassing, honestly, because I knew I wasn’t handling it right, but in the moment it felt like I couldn’t keep it down. It’s not like I’m throwing things or trying to start fights, but yeah… I’m more irritable, and it’s been coming out at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s more like some things feel harder to get myself to do, or I don’t know if I’m in the mood, but I can’t tell if that’s the same thing as not enjoying them anymore.\n\nLike, if you mean do I still like the things I used to like in general… I think so? I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I don’t care about any of this now.” It’s not that clear for me. Some days I still want normal stuff, and other days I just kind of don’t feel like dealing with anything.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed a big loss of interest across the board. I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed about what happened when the bluff gave way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… ashamed, definitely. Guilty too, but the shame is probably the bigger thing. It’s like this constant feeling under everything most days, even if I’m acting normal or trying to get through work or whatever.\n\nI keep feeling embarrassed, like people can somehow tell just by looking at me that I messed up. Like they know I was out there and I made the wrong choice, or I wasn’t paying attention, or I wasn’t as careful as I should’ve been. I know that probably sounds kind of irrational, because most people don’t even know the details, but it still feels that way. Like I’m walking around with it written on me.\n\nAnd it’s also… I used to act tougher than I probably was. I don’t mean like picking fights or anything, just, I’d kind of carry myself like I could handle things, like I wasn’t the kind of person who got scared like that. Since the bluff gave way, I don’t feel like that anymore. Most days I feel scared underneath, and embarrassed that I’m scared. Like I’m not who I thought I was, or not who I tried to make people think I was.\n\nSo yeah, I feel ashamed about it a lot. I try not to show it, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing things you used to enjoy since the bluff gave way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, the big one is hiking. It’s like my body reacts before I’ve even really thought about it.\n\nMy hiking buddies still text sometimes, and I know they’re just trying to include me, but I dodge the messages. I’ll see the notification and just leave it there, or tell myself I’ll answer later, and then I don’t. Because even talking about going back out there makes my chest tighten. Like just the idea of making a plan, looking at a map, thinking about being near that edge again… I can’t do it.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I have stopped doing that. It’s not that I don’t miss it, because part of me does. But I avoid it now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly it’s the coast stuff, honestly. Like… anything that feels connected to those trails, even if it’s not technically “hiking.” I don’t go near the coast trails now at all. I’ll take a different route if I think I might end up around there, or I’ll just say I’m busy if someone brings it up.

And the thing with my hiking buddies is part of it too. I know that’s not exactly an “activity” in the same way, but I avoid the whole social side of it. If they text about going out there, or even just send something like, “We should get back on the trail sometime,” I kind of freeze. I’ll see the message pop up and my chest tightens right away, before I’ve even opened it. Then I just don’t answer, or I wait so long that it feels too awkward to answer.

So yeah, besides the actual hiking, I avoid talking about it, planning it, being near the area… basically anything that puts me back in that headspace.

I’ll see the message pop up and I know I should say something, but even talking about going back out there makes me feel like I can’t breathe right. So I dodge it. I leave them on read or make up some excuse later. It’s easier than trying to explain why I can’t even handle the idea of being near those trails anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these sleep disturbances and distressing dreams?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months now, I think. Basically since the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped there. Before that, I mean, I had normal bad nights sometimes like anyone does, but nothing like this.\n\nIt started right after that happened, and at first I kept thinking it would settle down once some time passed, like my body just needed to calm down or whatever. But it’s just kept going. So yeah… around four months of the sleep being messed up and those dreams happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on guard or easily startled, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I know I’ve been tense in general, but “constantly on guard” sounds really specific, and I don’t know if I’d say I’ve noticed it that clearly.\n\nLike, there are times where I’ll feel jumpy or like I’m bracing for something, but I can’t tell if that’s what you mean or if I’m just stressed and not sleeping well. I haven’t been keeping track of it the way I’ve noticed the dreams or avoiding the trails. If someone surprises me, I might react, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or not. It’s kind of hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts and memories of the bluff giving way when they come up during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to let it get going in my head, because once it starts, it feels like it kind of takes over. Like if something reminds me of that day, I’ll try to shut it down fast—change what I’m doing, look at my phone, put on a video, anything to not sit there thinking about the bluff giving way.\n\nAnd it’s not just the actual place, though that’s the biggest thing. I won’t go near the coast trails now. Even the idea of being close to them makes me feel like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it. And when my hiking buddies text me, even if they’re just talking about going out or mentioning trails, I dodge it. I’ll see the message and just leave it there because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad, because they’re not doing anything wrong. They’re just trying to include me or check in. But if I answer, then I feel like I’ll have to talk about it, or explain why I can’t go, and then it brings the whole thing back up. So I just avoid the message, avoid the conversation, avoid anything that points my mind back there. It’s easier in the moment, even if I know I’m kind of making my life smaller by doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself scanning your surroundings for potential dangers, even in safe environments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m scanning for danger, exactly. Like, I get what you mean, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed myself doing that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I might look around more if I’m somewhere that makes me uncomfortable, but in regular safe places? I haven’t really caught myself checking exits or watching every person or anything like that. At least not that I can point to and say, “Yeah, that’s definitely happening.” Sometimes I’m just tense and distracted, so maybe I’m more aware of things around me, but I don’t know if that counts as looking for danger.\n\nI guess my honest answer is… I’m not sure. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other stuff has.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because they remind you of the bluff giving way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly with my hiking friends. I used to go out with them, or at least talk about plans and trails and stuff, and now I just kind of… avoid all of it.\n\nI won’t go near the coast trails anymore. Like, even thinking about being out there again makes me tense up, and if somebody brings up going near that area, I don’t want any part of the conversation. And when my hiking buddies text me, I’ll see it come in and just not answer, because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten. It’s like if I respond, then I’m opening the door to having to explain why I don’t want to go, or having to think about the bluff giving way again, and I just don’t want to go there.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve kind of pulled back from them. Not because I don’t care about them or anything. It’s more that the whole hiking thing is tied to it now, and being around that conversation feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're waiting for something bad to happen, even when everything seems okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s hard to explain because it’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “Something bad is about to happen,” but it’s kind of running underneath everything.\n\nSince the bluff gave way, I keep having this thought that I was stupid for being out there alone. Like, I replay it and think, why did I go there by myself, why didn’t I think more, why was I so careless? And then that turns into this bigger feeling that if I’m not paying attention, if I let my guard down even a little, something bad could happen again.\n\nEven when things are technically fine, I don’t really feel settled. Like I can be at work or just doing normal stuff, and there’s this sense that I shouldn’t get too comfortable. It feels like the world is more dangerous than I thought, or like I already proved I can make one bad decision and then everything can go wrong really fast. So yeah, I guess I am kind of waiting for something bad to happen, even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable or have outbursts of anger?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, more than I’d like to admit. I don’t think of myself as, like, an angry person normally, but lately my patience is just way shorter, especially at work.\n\nI do customer service, so people are already upset half the time, and before I could usually just kind of take it and stay polite, even if they were being rude. But now it’s like I hit that point so much faster. Someone will complain about something small or keep repeating themselves, and I can feel myself getting hot, like I’m already at the end of my rope. And I’ve snapped at customers over little things that I probably should’ve just let go.\n\nThere were a couple times where I raised my voice enough that my supervisor had to pull me aside afterward. That was embarrassing, honestly, because I knew I wasn’t handling it right, but in the moment it felt like I couldn’t keep it down. It’s not like I’m throwing things or trying to start fights, but yeah… I’m more irritable, and it’s been coming out at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s more like some things feel harder to get myself to do, or I don’t know if I’m in the mood, but I can’t tell if that’s the same thing as not enjoying them anymore.\n\nLike, if you mean do I still like the things I used to like in general… I think so? I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I don’t care about any of this now.” It’s not that clear for me. Some days I still want normal stuff, and other days I just kind of don’t feel like dealing with anything.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t really noticed a big loss of interest across the board. I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed about what happened when the bluff gave way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… ashamed, definitely. Guilty too, but the shame is probably the bigger thing. It’s like this constant feeling under everything most days, even if I’m acting normal or trying to get through work or whatever.\n\nI keep feeling embarrassed, like people can somehow tell just by looking at me that I messed up. Like they know I was out there and I made the wrong choice, or I wasn’t paying attention, or I wasn’t as careful as I should’ve been. I know that probably sounds kind of irrational, because most people don’t even know the details, but it still feels that way. Like I’m walking around with it written on me.\n\nAnd it’s also… I used to act tougher than I probably was. I don’t mean like picking fights or anything, just, I’d kind of carry myself like I could handle things, like I wasn’t the kind of person who got scared like that. Since the bluff gave way, I don’t feel like that anymore. Most days I feel scared underneath, and embarrassed that I’m scared. Like I’m not who I thought I was, or not who I tried to make people think I was.\n\nSo yeah, I feel ashamed about it a lot. I try not to show it, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing things you used to enjoy since the bluff gave way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, the big one is hiking. Or anything near those coast trails, really.\n\nI used to go out there with friends, like not every single day or anything, but it was one of the things I actually looked forward to. We'd make plans, talk about which trail to do, send pictures back and forth, all that. And now I just… I won’t go near it. If someone even mentions the coast trails, I feel myself kind of shut down. It’s like my body reacts before I’ve even really thought about it.\n\nMy hiking buddies still text sometimes, and I know they’re just trying to include me, but I dodge the messages. I’ll see the notification and just leave it there, or tell myself I’ll answer later, and then I don’t. Because even talking about going back out there makes my chest tighten. Like just the idea of making a plan, looking at a map, thinking about being near that edge again… I can’t do it.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I have stopped doing that. It’s not that I don’t miss it, because part of me does. But I avoid it now.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided other activities besides hiking since the bluff gave way?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the coast stuff, honestly. Like… anything that feels connected to those trails, even if it’s not technically “hiking.” I don’t go near the coast trails now at all. I’ll take a different route if I think I might end up around there, or I’ll just say I’m busy if someone brings it up.\n\nAnd the thing with my hiking buddies is part of it too. I know that’s not exactly an “activity” in the same way, but I avoid the whole social side of it. If they text about going out there, or even just send something like, “We should get back on the trail sometime,” I kind of freeze. I’ll see the message pop up and my chest tightens right away, before I’ve even opened it. Then I just don’t answer, or I wait so long that it feels too awkward to answer.\n\nSo yeah, besides the actual hiking, I avoid talking about it, planning it, being near the area… basically anything that puts me back in that headspace.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I know that probably sounds kind of irrational, because most people don’t even know the details, but it still feels that way. Like I’m walking around with it written on me.\n\nAnd it’s also… I used to act tougher than I probably was. I don’t mean like picking fights or anything, just, I’d kind of carry myself like I could handle things, like I wasn’t the kind of person who got scared like that. Since the bluff gave way, I don’t feel like that anymore. Most days I feel scared underneath, and embarrassed that I’m scared. Like I’m not who I thought I was, or not who I tried to make people think I was.\n\nSo yeah, I feel ashamed about it a lot. I try not to show it, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped doing things you used to enjoy since the bluff gave way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, the big one is hiking. Or anything near those coast trails, really.\n\nI used to go out there with friends, like not every single day or anything, but it was one of the things I actually looked forward to. We'd make plans, talk about which trail to do, send pictures back and forth, all that. And now I just… I won’t go near it. If someone even mentions the coast trails, I feel myself kind of shut down. It’s like my body reacts before I’ve even really thought about it.\n\nMy hiking buddies still text sometimes, and I know they’re just trying to include me, but I dodge the messages. I’ll see the notification and just leave it there, or tell myself I’ll answer later, and then I don’t. Because even talking about going back out there makes my chest tighten. Like just the idea of making a plan, looking at a map, thinking about being near that edge again… I can’t do it.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I have stopped doing that. It’s not that I don’t miss it, because part of me does. But I avoid it now.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided other activities besides hiking since the bluff gave way?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the coast stuff, honestly. Like… anything that feels connected to those trails, even if it’s not technically “hiking.” I don’t go near the coast trails now at all. I’ll take a different route if I think I might end up around there, or I’ll just say I’m busy if someone brings it up.\n\nAnd the thing with my hiking buddies is part of it too. I know that’s not exactly an “activity” in the same way, but I avoid the whole social side of it. If they text about going out there, or even just send something like, “We should get back on the trail sometime,” I kind of freeze. I’ll see the message pop up and my chest tightens right away, before I’ve even opened it. Then I just don’t answer, or I wait so long that it feels too awkward to answer.\n\nSo yeah, besides the actual hiking, I avoid talking about it, planning it, being near the area… basically anything that puts me back in that headspace.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it’s hard for me to separate “lost interest” from just… not wanting to deal with certain things anymore.

Like, I know with hiking and the coast stuff, I avoid it. That part is obvious. But if you mean everything in general, like all the stuff I used to like, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest across the board. It’s more like some things don’t feel as easy to enjoy, if that makes sense. I’ll start something and kind of feel distracted or not as into it, but then I wonder if that’s just because I’m tired or in my head.

I still can enjoy some normal stuff sometimes. It’s not like nothing ever feels good. But with things that remind me of being out there, or things that used to be tied to that part of my life, it’s different. I don’t get excited the way I used to. I’ll see people making plans and there’s this weird mix of missing it and not wanting anything to do with it.

So… I guess I’m not sure if I’ve lost interest, exactly. Some of it is still there, but it’s buried under a lot of other feelings.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I’m just not sleeping right anymore. Like, I’ll go to bed and I’m exhausted, because I’ve been at the call center all day trying to sound normal on the phone, but then I just lie there. If it’s raining, or even if I just start thinking about it, my mind goes straight to that bluff giving way, and I can’t shut it off.\n\nAnd then when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. I wake up sweaty from dreams, kind of startled, and then I have to get up and go to work like I rested, but I feel like I never slept at all. It’s been wearing me down, and I didn’t really know what else to do, so… that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned waking up sweaty from dreams. Are these dreams disturbing or frightening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… they’re disturbing. I mean, I don’t always remember the whole dream like a movie or anything, but I wake up soaked and my heart’s going, and it’s like my head was right back there with the bluff giving way. Sometimes it’s just the sound of rain in the dream, or that feeling like the ground isn’t solid under me, and then I’m awake before I even fully understand what happened.\n\nAnd then I just lie there afterward, staring around my room, trying to remind myself I’m not there. But by then I’m too keyed up to sleep again, so I end up going into the call center the next day feeling like I didn’t sleep at all, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or conversations because they remind you of the bluff giving way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I didn’t really think of it as “avoiding” at first, I just kept telling myself I didn’t feel like going, or I was busy, or the weather wasn’t good. But honestly, I won’t go near the coast trails now. Like, even if someone mentions taking a walk out that way, my stomach kind of drops and my chest tightens up.\n\nAnd my hiking buddies still text sometimes, like asking if I want to come out or just talking about routes, and I’ll just… not answer. I’ll see the message pop up and I know I should say something, but even talking about going back out there makes me feel like I can’t breathe right. So I dodge it. I leave them on read or make up some excuse later. It’s easier than trying to explain why I can’t even handle the idea of being near those trails anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these sleep disturbances and distressing dreams?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months now, I think. Basically since the bluff trail collapsed and I got trapped there. Before that, I mean, I had normal bad nights sometimes like anyone does, but nothing like this.\n\nIt started right after that happened, and at first I kept thinking it would settle down once some time passed, like my body just needed to calm down or whatever. But it’s just kept going. So yeah… around four months of the sleep being messed up and those dreams happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on guard or easily startled, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I know I’ve been tense in general, but “constantly on guard” sounds really specific, and I don’t know if I’d say I’ve noticed it that clearly.\n\nLike, there are times where I’ll feel jumpy or like I’m bracing for something, but I can’t tell if that’s what you mean or if I’m just stressed and not sleeping well. I haven’t been keeping track of it the way I’ve noticed the dreams or avoiding the trails. If someone surprises me, I might react, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal or not. It’s kind of hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thoughts and memories of the bluff giving way when they come up during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to let it get going in my head, because once it starts, it feels like it kind of takes over. Like if something reminds me of that day, I’ll try to shut it down fast—change what I’m doing, look at my phone, put on a video, anything to not sit there thinking about the bluff giving way.\n\nAnd it’s not just the actual place, though that’s the biggest thing. I won’t go near the coast trails now. Even the idea of being close to them makes me feel like my body reacts before I can talk myself out of it. And when my hiking buddies text me, even if they’re just talking about going out or mentioning trails, I dodge it. I’ll see the message and just leave it there because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad, because they’re not doing anything wrong. They’re just trying to include me or check in. But if I answer, then I feel like I’ll have to talk about it, or explain why I can’t go, and then it brings the whole thing back up. So I just avoid the message, avoid the conversation, avoid anything that points my mind back there. It’s easier in the moment, even if I know I’m kind of making my life smaller by doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself scanning your surroundings for potential dangers, even in safe environments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I’m scanning for danger, exactly. Like, I get what you mean, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed myself doing that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I might look around more if I’m somewhere that makes me uncomfortable, but in regular safe places? I haven’t really caught myself checking exits or watching every person or anything like that. At least not that I can point to and say, “Yeah, that’s definitely happening.” Sometimes I’m just tense and distracted, so maybe I’m more aware of things around me, but I don’t know if that counts as looking for danger.\n\nI guess my honest answer is… I’m not sure. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other stuff has.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or relationships because they remind you of the bluff giving way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly with my hiking friends. I used to go out with them, or at least talk about plans and trails and stuff, and now I just kind of… avoid all of it.\n\nI won’t go near the coast trails anymore. Like, even thinking about being out there again makes me tense up, and if somebody brings up going near that area, I don’t want any part of the conversation. And when my hiking buddies text me, I’ll see it come in and just not answer, because even talking about going out there makes my chest tighten. It’s like if I respond, then I’m opening the door to having to explain why I don’t want to go, or having to think about the bluff giving way again, and I just don’t want to go there.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’ve kind of pulled back from them. Not because I don’t care about them or anything. It’s more that the whole hiking thing is tied to it now, and being around that conversation feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're waiting for something bad to happen, even when everything seems okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s hard to explain because it’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “Something bad is about to happen,” but it’s kind of running underneath everything.\n\nSince the bluff gave way, I keep having this thought that I was stupid for being out there alone. Like, I replay it and think, why did I go there by myself, why didn’t I think more, why was I so careless? And then that turns into this bigger feeling that if I’m not paying attention, if I let my guard down even a little, something bad could happen again.\n\nEven when things are technically fine, I don’t really feel settled. Like I can be at work or just doing normal stuff, and there’s this sense that I shouldn’t get too comfortable. It feels like the world is more dangerous than I thought, or like I already proved I can make one bad decision and then everything can go wrong really fast. So yeah, I guess I am kind of waiting for something bad to happen, even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable or have outbursts of anger?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, more than I’d like to admit. I don’t think of myself as, like, an angry person normally, but lately my patience is just way shorter, especially at work.\n\nI do customer service, so people are already upset half the time, and before I could usually just kind of take it and stay polite, even if they were being rude. But now it’s like I hit that point so much faster. Someone will complain about something small or keep repeating themselves, and I can feel myself getting hot, like I’m already at the end of my rope. And I’ve snapped at customers over little things that I probably should’ve just let go.\n\nThere were a couple times where I raised my voice enough that my supervisor had to pull me aside afterward. That was embarrassing, honestly, because I knew I wasn’t handling it right, but in the moment it felt like I couldn’t keep it down. It’s not like I’m throwing things or trying to start fights, but yeah… I’m more irritable, and it’s been coming out at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t know if I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. It’s more like some things feel harder to get myself to do, or I don’t know if I’m in the mood, but I can’t tell if that’s the same thing as not enjoying them anymore.\n\nLike, if you mean do I still like the things I used to like in general… I think so?
